========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hi doctor. Thank you for fitting me in. I, um... I'm here because things have just gotten completely out of hand at work. I'm a building cleaner, I've been doing janitorial work for a long time now, mostly in these older commercial buildings downtown. And... well, it's the rats. Or, really, even just the *thought* of seeing a rat. 

It has gotten so incredibly bad lately that I literally can't do parts of my job anymore. I mean, I'll be pushing my cart down to the basement level or heading toward the main trash room at the end of my shift, and I just... I can't go in. I stand there at the door and I just can't make myself do it. And the frustrating part—the really maddening part—is that I know better! I tell myself over and over, I know it’s probably just an old building, you know? It's just a trash room. It's not actually dangerous to go in there and empty the bins. 

But I just picture one scurrying out from under a dumpster, or I think I hear a scratch, and my whole body just refuses to move. I've had to start asking the new guy on my shift to cover the lower levels for me, making up excuses, and I can't keep doing that without getting fired. I've been at this job for years and I can't afford to lose it, but I just don't know how to stop being so terrified over something that probably isn't even there most of the time...

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, everywhere. It’s definitely not just at my job anymore, it’s really bleeding into my everyday life and dictating where I can and can't go. I mean, I will completely turn down any walking routes or going into places if they involve basements, or those creepy little storage rooms, or even communal laundry areas. Even alley dumpsters! Like, if I'm walking home from the grocery store or running errands, I will literally cross the street and walk three or four blocks out of my way just to avoid walking past an alley with a dumpster, because my mind just immediately goes to what might be hiding in there. 

I try to avoid them whenever I possibly can, but you know, sometimes you just can't. And if I can't avoid it—like if I’m absolutely forced to clean one of those areas, whether it's the laundry room at my own apartment building or one of the storage rooms on my route at work that I couldn't get the new guy to cover for me—I have to leave the door wide open. I'll find a brick or a heavy trash can, whatever I can find, really, just to wedge that door open so I know I have a clear escape route. And then I just rush through it. I'm practically running, just rushing through the sweeping or the mopping as fast as I possibly can, not even doing a very thorough job if I'm being honest, and I just feel completely scared the whole entire time. I'm just on edge, staring at the corners, waiting for something to dart out at my feet. It’s just so exhausting, doctor, having to constantly map out my entire day just to avoid a laundry room or a dumpster, and then being absolutely terrified when I'm forced to deal with one.

Like, if I'm walking home from the grocery store or running errands, I will literally cross the street and walk three or four blocks out of my way just to avoid walking past an alley with a dumpster, because my mind just immediately goes to what might be hiding in there. \n\nI try to avoid them whenever I possibly can, but you know, sometimes you just can't. And if I can't avoid it—like if I’m absolutely forced to clean one of those areas, whether it's the laundry room at my own apartment building or one of the storage rooms on my route at work that I couldn't get the new guy to cover for me—I have to leave the door wide open. I'll find a brick or a heavy trash can, whatever I can find, really, just to wedge that door open so I know I have a clear escape route. And then I just rush through it. I'm practically running, just rushing through the sweeping or the mopping as fast as I possibly can, not even doing a very thorough job if I'm being honest, and I just feel completely scared the whole entire time. I'm just on edge, staring at the corners, waiting for something to dart out at my feet. It’s just so exhausting, doctor, having to constantly map out my entire day just to avoid a laundry room or a dumpster, and then being absolutely terrified when I'm forced to deal with one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, it's really just the rats. I mean, I'm not a fan of spiders or anything, but who is, right? If I see a spider, I just grab a paper towel and squish it, or I'll ask someone else to get it if it's a big one. It's nothing like this. And mice... well, mice are gross, sure, but they don't give me that same absolute panic. I can handle a mouse trap if I absolutely have to, though I prefer not to. 

But rats... it's just something about them. The way they look, the way they move, those long tails... just thinking about it makes my skin crawl. I don't know what it is, honestly. I've never been bitten by one or anything like that. I didn't have some traumatic childhood experience with a pet rat or anything. It's just this deep, overpowering terror that I can't shake. 

Like, the other day, I saw a squirrel run across the sidewalk, and for a split second, my brain registered "rat" and my heart just stopped. I literally froze in my tracks until I realized what it was. And pigeons, you know, people call them rats with wings, but they don't bother me at all. I can sit on a park bench and eat my lunch with pigeons walking right up to my shoes. So it's very specific. It's just rats. Nothing else makes me feel like my chest is caving in and I need to run for my life. Just them.

I mean, I'm not a fan of spiders or anything, but who is, right? If I see a spider, I just grab a paper towel and squish it, or I'll ask someone else to get it if it's a big one. It's nothing like this. And mice... well, mice are gross, sure, but they don't give me that same absolute panic. I can handle a mouse trap if I absolutely have to, though I prefer not to. \n\nBut rats... it's just something about them. The way they look, the way they move, those long tails... just thinking about it makes my skin crawl. I don't know what it is, honestly. I've never been bitten by one or anything like that. I didn't have some traumatic childhood experience with a pet rat or anything. It's just this deep, overpowering terror that I can't shake. \n\nLike, the other day, I saw a squirrel run across the sidewalk, and for a split second, my brain registered \"rat\" and my heart just stopped. I literally froze in my tracks until I realized what it was. And pigeons, you know, people call them rats with wings, but they don't bother me at all. I can sit on a park bench and eat my lunch with pigeons walking right up to my shoes. So it's very specific. It's just rats. Nothing else makes me feel like my chest is caving in and I need to run for my life. Just them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, I guess not directly? Like, nobody has stopped talking to me because I'm afraid of rats, if that's what you mean. My family knows about it, obviously. My sister, she thinks I'm just being dramatic, which is so frustrating because she doesn't understand that it's not a choice. I'm not just acting out for attention, you know? And my friends... well, my friends are mostly pretty understanding, I suppose. 

But it does cause problems, just because of how much I have to avoid things. Like, last month, my friend Sarah invited me over for a barbecue at her new place. It was a really nice house, but to get to the backyard, you had to walk down this side path that was right next to a row of big, metal dumpsters for the apartment building next door. And I just... I couldn't do it. I stood at the front of her house, looking down that path, and I knew, I just *knew* there were rats in those dumpsters. I could practically hear them. So I called her and made up some excuse about a sudden migraine and went home. I felt terrible about it, and she was really disappointed. I think she knew I was lying, too. 

And then there's my husband. He tries to be patient, he really does, but I know it wears on him. Like, if we're out walking in the evening, I'll suddenly grab his arm and drag him across the street because I see a shadow near a storm drain. Or I'll refuse to go down to our own basement to get the holiday decorations, so he has to do it all by himself. He never complains, but I can see the look on his face sometimes, just this... exhaustion. Like he's tired of having to accommodate this ridiculous fear of mine. So, I don't know if it's ruining my relationships or anything, but it definitely puts a strain on things. It makes me feel like a burden, honestly, having to constantly explain why I can't go certain places or do certain things. It's just embarrassing, having to admit that a grown woman is terrified of a rodent.

Like, if I'm walking home from the grocery store or running errands, I will literally cross the street and walk three or four blocks out of my way just to avoid walking past an alley with a dumpster, because my mind just immediately goes to what might be hiding in there. \n\nI try to avoid them whenever I possibly can, but you know, sometimes you just can't. And if I can't avoid it—like if I’m absolutely forced to clean one of those areas, whether it's the laundry room at my own apartment building or one of the storage rooms on my route at work that I couldn't get the new guy to cover for me—I have to leave the door wide open. I'll find a brick or a heavy trash can, whatever I can find, really, just to wedge that door open so I know I have a clear escape route. And then I just rush through it. I'm practically running, just rushing through the sweeping or the mopping as fast as I possibly can, not even doing a very thorough job if I'm being honest, and I just feel completely scared the whole entire time. I'm just on edge, staring at the corners, waiting for something to dart out at my feet. It’s just so exhausting, doctor, having to constantly map out my entire day just to avoid a laundry room or a dumpster, and then being absolutely terrified when I'm forced to deal with one.\n\nDoctor: Is it just rats that you're afraid of, or are there other animals or things that trigger a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's really just the rats. I mean, I'm not a fan of spiders or anything, but who is, right? If I see a spider, I just grab a paper towel and squish it, or I'll ask someone else to get it if it's a big one. It's nothing like this. And mice... well, mice are gross, sure, but they don't give me that same absolute panic. I can handle a mouse trap if I absolutely have to, though I prefer not to. \n\nBut rats... it's just something about them. The way they look, the way they move, those long tails... just thinking about it makes my skin crawl. I don't know what it is, honestly. I've never been bitten by one or anything like that. I didn't have some traumatic childhood experience with a pet rat or anything. It's just this deep, overpowering terror that I can't shake. \n\nLike, the other day, I saw a squirrel run across the sidewalk, and for a split second, my brain registered \"rat\" and my heart just stopped. I literally froze in my tracks until I realized what it was. And pigeons, you know, people call them rats with wings, but they don't bother me at all. I can sit on a park bench and eat my lunch with pigeons walking right up to my shoes. So it's very specific. It's just rats. Nothing else makes me feel like my chest is caving in and I need to run for my life. Just them.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I guess not directly? Like, nobody has stopped talking to me because I'm afraid of rats, if that's what you mean. My family knows about it, obviously. My sister, she thinks I'm just being dramatic, which is so frustrating because she doesn't understand that it's not a choice. I'm not just acting out for attention, you know? And my friends... well, my friends are mostly pretty understanding, I suppose. \n\nBut it does cause problems, just because of how much I have to avoid things. Like, last month, my friend Sarah invited me over for a barbecue at her new place. It was a really nice house, but to get to the backyard, you had to walk down this side path that was right next to a row of big, metal dumpsters for the apartment building next door. And I just... I couldn't do it. I stood at the front of her house, looking down that path, and I knew, I just *knew* there were rats in those dumpsters. I could practically hear them. So I called her and made up some excuse about a sudden migraine and went home. I felt terrible about it, and she was really disappointed. I think she knew I was lying, too. \n\nAnd then there's my husband. He tries to be patient, he really does, but I know it wears on him. Like, if we're out walking in the evening, I'll suddenly grab his arm and drag him across the street because I see a shadow near a storm drain. Or I'll refuse to go down to our own basement to get the holiday decorations, so he has to do it all by himself. He never complains, but I can see the look on his face sometimes, just this... exhaustion. Like he's tired of having to accommodate this ridiculous fear of mine. So, I don't know if it's ruining my relationships or anything, but it definitely puts a strain on things. It makes me feel like a burden, honestly, having to constantly explain why I can't go certain places or do certain things. It's just embarrassing, having to admit that a grown woman is terrified of a rodent.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. Yes. It's not a slow build-up, you know? It's not like I start getting a little nervous and then it gets worse. It is instant. It's like flipping a switch. 

Just last week, I was cleaning the first-floor hallway of this one building on my route—an older building, lots of exposed pipes and weird little nooks. I was just mopping, minding my own business, thinking about what I was going to make for dinner, when I heard this... scratching sound. Just a tiny, little scratch-scratch-scratch coming from behind the baseboard near the radiator pipe. And I didn't even see anything! I just heard the sound, and my brain instantly screamed *rat*. 

My whole body just reacted right away. It's like I couldn't even control it. My heart started pounding so hard and so fast I thought it was going to actually crack my ribs. I could hear my pulse roaring in my ears. And my hands... my hands started shaking so violently I actually dropped the mop handle. It just clattered to the floor. I couldn't breathe right, my chest got all tight, and I just had this overwhelming, blinding urge that I had to get out of there. Like, right that second. Before I could even think straight or rationalize that it might just be the pipes settling or a mouse or something, I was already backing away toward the heavy glass front doors. 

It happens if I see something move out of the corner of my eye near a pipe, or if I hear a rustle in a trash bag I'm about to tie up. It's this sudden, massive wave of pure panic that just crashes over me in seconds. I get dizzy, I sweat, and I just feel this absolute, terrifying certainty that if I don't run away immediately, something horrible is going to happen to me. It takes me sometimes twenty or thirty minutes just sitting in my car with the doors locked and the AC blasting to finally get my breathing back to normal and stop shaking after something like that happens. It's just awful, doctor. It really is.

Like, last month, my friend Sarah invited me over for a barbecue at her new place. It was a really nice house, but to get to the backyard, you had to walk down this side path that was right next to a row of big, metal dumpsters for the apartment building next door. And I just... I couldn't do it. I stood at the front of her house, looking down that path, and I knew, I just *knew* there were rats in those dumpsters. I could practically hear them. So I called her and made up some excuse about a sudden migraine and went home. I felt terrible about it, and she was really disappointed. I think she knew I was lying, too. \n\nAnd then there's my husband. He tries to be patient, he really does, but I know it wears on him. Like, if we're out walking in the evening, I'll suddenly grab his arm and drag him across the street because I see a shadow near a storm drain. Or I'll refuse to go down to our own basement to get the holiday decorations, so he has to do it all by himself. He never complains, but I can see the look on his face sometimes, just this... exhaustion. Like he's tired of having to accommodate this ridiculous fear of mine. So, I don't know if it's ruining my relationships or anything, but it definitely puts a strain on things. It makes me feel like a burden, honestly, having to constantly explain why I can't go certain places or do certain things. It's just embarrassing, having to admit that a grown woman is terrified of a rodent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, like a panic attack, when you thought about or encountered rats?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yes. It's not a slow build-up, you know? It's not like I start getting a little nervous and then it gets worse. It is instant. It's like flipping a switch. \n\nJust last week, I was cleaning the first-floor hallway of this one building on my route—an older building, lots of exposed pipes and weird little nooks. I was just mopping, minding my own business, thinking about what I was going to make for dinner, when I heard this... scratching sound. Just a tiny, little scratch-scratch-scratch coming from behind the baseboard near the radiator pipe. And I didn't even see anything! I just heard the sound, and my brain instantly screamed *rat*. \n\nMy whole body just reacted right away. It's like I couldn't even control it. My heart started pounding so hard and so fast I thought it was going to actually crack my ribs. I could hear my pulse roaring in my ears. And my hands... my hands started shaking so violently I actually dropped the mop handle. It just clattered to the floor. I couldn't breathe right, my chest got all tight, and I just had this overwhelming, blinding urge that I had to get out of there. Like, right that second. Before I could even think straight or rationalize that it might just be the pipes settling or a mouse or something, I was already backing away toward the heavy glass front doors. \n\nIt happens if I see something move out of the corner of my eye near a pipe, or if I hear a rustle in a trash bag I'm about to tie up. It's this sudden, massive wave of pure panic that just crashes over me in seconds. I get dizzy, I sweat, and I just feel this absolute, terrifying certainty that if I don't run away immediately, something horrible is going to happen to me. It takes me sometimes twenty or thirty minutes just sitting in my car with the doors locked and the AC blasting to finally get my breathing back to normal and stop shaking after something like that happens. It's just awful, doctor. It really is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, it’s definitely the possibility. Honestly, just the thought that they *might* be there is enough to completely dictate how I do my job. You know, being a building cleaner, you get assigned all sorts of different zones or routes, and I’ve had to get really, really strict about what I’ll accept. If my supervisor tries to hand me a schedule that includes basements, or those creepy, cluttered storage rooms where people just dump old mattresses and cardboard boxes... I just turn them down. I have to. I turn down any routes with laundry areas, too, especially in those older buildings where the machines are down in some damp cellar, or if the job means I have to take the heavy bags out to the alley dumpsters. I avoid all of it whenever I possibly can. I'll make up excuses, I'll trade shifts with the newer girls, whatever it takes. 

But, you know, sometimes you just don't have a choice. Sometimes we're short-staffed, or there's a massive spill that has to be dealt with right then and there, or the boss is watching, and I'm forced to go clean one of those areas. And when that happens... it is just agonizing. I'm terrified before I even walk down the stairs. If I absolutely have to go in, I always, always keep the door propped wide open. I actually carry this heavy rubber wedge in my supply cart just for that, so I know I have a clear escape route and I won't get trapped down there. 

And then I just rush through the work. I mean, I am practically running with the mop. I don't look in the dark corners, I don't move anything I don't have to, I just do the bare minimum as fast as humanly possible to get out of there. I feel so scared the whole time, just this awful, tight knot in my stomach, jumping at every little shadow or creak the building makes. I'm just waiting for something to dart out at my boots. So, yeah, I don't even need to see an actual rat. Just knowing it's a place where they *could* be is enough to make me an absolute nervous wreck.

I'll find a brick or a heavy trash can, whatever I can find, really, just to wedge that door open so I know I have a clear escape route. And then I just rush through it. I'm practically running, just rushing through the sweeping or the mopping as fast as I possibly can, not even doing a very thorough job if I'm being honest, and I just feel completely scared the whole entire time. I'm just on edge, staring at the corners, waiting for something to dart out at my feet. It’s just so exhausting, doctor, having to constantly map out my entire day just to avoid a laundry room or a dumpster, and then being absolutely terrified when I'm forced to deal with one.\n\nDoctor: Is it just rats that you're afraid of, or are there other animals or things that trigger a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's really just the rats. I mean, I'm not a fan of spiders or anything, but who is, right? If I see a spider, I just grab a paper towel and squish it, or I'll ask someone else to get it if it's a big one. It's nothing like this. And mice... well, mice are gross, sure, but they don't give me that same absolute panic. I can handle a mouse trap if I absolutely have to, though I prefer not to. \n\nBut rats... it's just something about them. The way they look, the way they move, those long tails... just thinking about it makes my skin crawl. I don't know what it is, honestly. I've never been bitten by one or anything like that. I didn't have some traumatic childhood experience with a pet rat or anything. It's just this deep, overpowering terror that I can't shake. \n\nLike, the other day, I saw a squirrel run across the sidewalk, and for a split second, my brain registered \"rat\" and my heart just stopped. I literally froze in my tracks until I realized what it was. And pigeons, you know, people call them rats with wings, but they don't bother me at all. I can sit on a park bench and eat my lunch with pigeons walking right up to my shoes. So it's very specific. It's just rats. Like, nobody has stopped talking to me because I'm afraid of rats, if that's what you mean. My family knows about it, obviously. And my friends... well, my friends are mostly pretty understanding, I suppose. \n\nBut it does cause problems, just because of how much I have to avoid things. Like, last month, my friend Sarah invited me over for a barbecue at her new place. It was a really nice house, but to get to the backyard, you had to walk down this side path that was right next to a row of big, metal dumpsters for the apartment building next door. And I just... I couldn't do it. I stood at the front of her house, looking down that path, and I knew, I just *knew* there were rats in those dumpsters. I could practically hear them. So I called her and made up some excuse about a sudden migraine and went home. I felt terrible about it, and she was really disappointed. I think she knew I was lying, too. \n\nAnd then there's my husband. He tries to be patient, he really does, but I know it wears on him. Like, if we're out walking in the evening, I'll suddenly grab his arm and drag him across the street because I see a shadow near a storm drain.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Specific Phobia. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).\n  manifestation: I’m here because the thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a trash room and not really dangerous.\n- key: S063\n  name: Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response\n  description: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: If I hear scratching near a wall or see something move by a pipe, my whole body reacts right away—my heart pounds, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get out before I can even think straight.\n- key: S064\n  name: Phobic_Avoidance_Endure\n  description: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: I turn down routes with basements, storage rooms, laundry areas, or alley dumpsters when I can, and if I’m forced to clean one I keep the door open, rush through it, and feel scared the whole time.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve had to refuse or delay cleaning assignments in certain buildings, and my supervisor has started questioning whether I can be counted on for regular routes. Like, nobody has stopped talking to me because I'm afraid of rats, if that's what you mean. My family knows about it, obviously. My sister, she thinks I'm just being dramatic, which is so frustrating because she doesn't understand that it's not a choice. I'm not just acting out for attention, you know? Like, last month, my friend Sarah invited me over for a barbecue at her new place. It was a really nice house, but to get to the backyard, you had to walk down this side path that was right next to a row of big, metal dumpsters for the apartment building next door. And I just... I couldn't do it. I stood at the front of her house, looking down that path, and I knew, I just *knew* there were rats in those dumpsters. I could practically hear them. So I called her and made up some excuse about a sudden migraine and went home. I felt terrible about it, and she was really disappointed. I think she knew I was lying, too. \n\nAnd then there's my husband. He tries to be patient, he really does, but I know it wears on him. Like, if we're out walking in the evening, I'll suddenly grab his arm and drag him across the street because I see a shadow near a storm drain. Or I'll refuse to go down to our own basement to get the holiday decorations, so he has to do it all by himself. He never complains, but I can see the look on his face sometimes, just this... exhaustion. Like he's tired of having to accommodate this ridiculous fear of mine. So, I don't know if it's ruining my relationships or anything, but it definitely puts a strain on things. It makes me feel like a burden, honestly, having to constantly explain why I can't go certain places or do certain things. It's just embarrassing, having to admit that a grown woman is terrified of a rodent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, like a panic attack, when you thought about or encountered rats?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yes. It's not a slow build-up, you know? It's not like I start getting a little nervous and then it gets worse. It is instant. It's like flipping a switch. \n\nJust last week, I was cleaning the first-floor hallway of this one building on my route—an older building, lots of exposed pipes and weird little nooks. I was just mopping, minding my own business, thinking about what I was going to make for dinner, when I heard this... scratching sound. Just a tiny, little scratch-scratch-scratch coming from behind the baseboard near the radiator pipe. And I didn't even see anything! I just heard the sound, and my brain instantly screamed *rat*. \n\nMy whole body just reacted right away. It's like I couldn't even control it. My heart started pounding so hard and so fast I thought it was going to actually crack my ribs. I could hear my pulse roaring in my ears. And my hands... my hands started shaking so violently I actually dropped the mop handle. It just clattered to the floor. I couldn't breathe right, my chest got all tight, and I just had this overwhelming, blinding urge that I had to get out of there. Like, right that second. Before I could even think straight or rationalize that it might just be the pipes settling or a mouse or something, I was already backing away toward the heavy glass front doors. \n\nIt happens if I see something move out of the corner of my eye near a pipe, or if I hear a rustle in a trash bag I'm about to tie up. It's this sudden, massive wave of pure panic that just crashes over me in seconds. I get dizzy, I sweat, and I just feel this absolute, terrifying certainty that if I don't run away immediately, something horrible is going to happen to me. It takes me sometimes twenty or thirty minutes just sitting in my car with the doors locked and the AC blasting to finally get my breathing back to normal and stop shaking after something like that happens. It's just awful, doctor. It really is.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the actual presence of rats or also by the possibility that rats might be in a particular location?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely the possibility. Honestly, just the thought that they *might* be there is enough to completely dictate how I do my job. You know, being a building cleaner, you get assigned all sorts of different zones or routes, and I’ve had to get really, really strict about what I’ll accept. If my supervisor tries to hand me a schedule that includes basements, or those creepy, cluttered storage rooms where people just dump old mattresses and cardboard boxes... I just turn them down. I have to. I turn down any routes with laundry areas, too, especially in those older buildings where the machines are down in some damp cellar, or if the job means I have to take the heavy bags out to the alley dumpsters. I avoid all of it whenever I possibly can. I'll make up excuses, I'll trade shifts with the newer girls, whatever it takes. \n\nBut, you know, sometimes you just don't have a choice. Sometimes we're short-staffed, or there's a massive spill that has to be dealt with right then and there, or the boss is watching, and I'm forced to go clean one of those areas. And when that happens... it is just agonizing. I'm terrified before I even walk down the stairs. If I absolutely have to go in, I always, always keep the door propped wide open. I actually carry this heavy rubber wedge in my supply cart just for that, so I know I have a clear escape route and I won't get trapped down there. \n\nAnd then I just rush through the work. I mean, I am practically running with the mop. I don't look in the dark corners, I don't move anything I don't have to, I just do the bare minimum as fast as humanly possible to get out of there. I feel so scared the whole time, just this awful, tight knot in my stomach, jumping at every little shadow or creak the building makes. I'm just waiting for something to dart out at my boots. So, yeah, I don't even need to see an actual rat. Just knowing it's a place where they *could* be is enough to make me an absolute nervous wreck.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever experienced a similar fear response when thinking about other rodents, like mice, even if you said they don't bother you as much as rats?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, actually. Not at all. It’s funny you ask that, because people always assume, you know, if you hate one rodent, you must hate them all. But mice? Mice really don't do that to me. I mean, they’re a nuisance, obviously, especially in my line of work, but they don't give me that... that blinding panic. 

Like, just to give you an example, maybe two or three years ago, I was cleaning out this old supply closet on the third floor of the municipal building. It was packed with old files and junk. I moved this heavy box of paper towels, and a tiny little field mouse just scurried right over the toe of my work boot. And you know what I did? I just jumped back a little, maybe let out a little gasp, and went, 'Shoo! Get out of here!' And that was it. My heart didn't start pounding, I didn't get dizzy, I didn't drop my things and run for the door. I just thought, 'Ugh, gross, I need to leave a note for maintenance to put down a trap.' It was just an annoyance. 

Because, I mean, mice are little, you know? They have those big round ears and they just want to hide from you. They're almost... well, I won't say cute, but they aren't monsters. But rats... rats are just entirely different. They're huge, they have those thick, scaly, disgusting tails, and they look at you like they aren't even scared of you. Like they'd just as soon bite you as run away. So no, thinking about mice doesn't bother me in the slightest. I can see a mouse on TV, or even think about one being in the wall, and I'm totally fine. 

Even squirrels! People say squirrels are just rats with fuzzy tails, right? But squirrels don't bother me one bit. I actually leave peanuts out for the squirrels on my back porch in the mornings. My husband thinks it's completely hilarious, given how I act about rats, but it's just not the same thing in my brain at all. It is exclusively, one hundred percent, just rats that make me feel like my world is ending.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi doctor. Thank you for fitting me in. I, um... I'm here because things have just gotten completely out of hand at work. I'm a building cleaner, I've been doing janitorial work for a long time now, mostly in these older commercial buildings downtown. And... well, it's the rats. Or, really, even just the *thought* of seeing a rat. \n\nIt has gotten so incredibly bad lately that I literally can't do parts of my job anymore. I mean, I'll be pushing my cart down to the basement level or heading toward the main trash room at the end of my shift, and I just... I can't go in. I stand there at the door and I just can't make myself do it. And the frustrating part—the really maddening part—is that I know better! I tell myself over and over, I know it’s probably just an old building, you know? It's just a trash room. It's not actually dangerous to go in there and empty the bins. \n\nBut I just picture one scurrying out from under a dumpster, or I think I hear a scratch, and my whole body just refuses to move. I've had to start asking the new guy on my shift to cover the lower levels for me, making up excuses, and I can't keep doing that without getting fired. I've been at this job for years and I can't afford to lose it, but I just don't know how to stop being so terrified over something that probably isn't even there most of the time...\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or places outside of work where you avoid going because you might see a rat?\n\nPatient: Oh, everywhere. It’s definitely not just at my job anymore, it’s really bleeding into my everyday life and dictating where I can and can't go. I mean, I will completely turn down any walking routes or going into places if they involve basements, or those creepy little storage rooms, or even communal laundry areas. Even alley dumpsters! Like, if I'm walking home from the grocery store or running errands, I will literally cross the street and walk three or four blocks out of my way just to avoid walking past an alley with a dumpster, because my mind just immediately goes to what might be hiding in there. \n\nI try to avoid them whenever I possibly can, but you know, sometimes you just can't. And if I can't avoid it—like if I’m absolutely forced to clean one of those areas, whether it's the laundry room at my own apartment building or one of the storage rooms on my route at work that I couldn't get the new guy to cover for me—I have to leave the door wide open. I'll find a brick or a heavy trash can, whatever I can find, really, just to wedge that door open so I know I have a clear escape route. And then I just rush through it. I'm practically running, just rushing through the sweeping or the mopping as fast as I possibly can, not even doing a very thorough job if I'm being honest, and I just feel completely scared the whole entire time. I'm just on edge, staring at the corners, waiting for something to dart out at my feet. It’s just so exhausting, doctor, having to constantly map out my entire day just to avoid a laundry room or a dumpster, and then being absolutely terrified when I'm forced to deal with one.\n\nDoctor: Is it just rats that you're afraid of, or are there other animals or things that trigger a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's really just the rats. I mean, I'm not a fan of spiders or anything, but who is, right? If I see a spider, I just grab a paper towel and squish it, or I'll ask someone else to get it if it's a big one. It's nothing like this. And mice... well, mice are gross, sure, but they don't give me that same absolute panic. I can handle a mouse trap if I absolutely have to, though I prefer not to. \n\nBut rats... it's just something about them. The way they look, the way they move, those long tails... just thinking about it makes my skin crawl. I don't know what it is, honestly. I've never been bitten by one or anything like that. I didn't have some traumatic childhood experience with a pet rat or anything. It's just this deep, overpowering terror that I can't shake. \n\nLike, the other day, I saw a squirrel run across the sidewalk, and for a split second, my brain registered \"rat\" and my heart just stopped. I literally froze in my tracks until I realized what it was. And pigeons, you know, people call them rats with wings, but they don't bother me at all. I can sit on a park bench and eat my lunch with pigeons walking right up to my shoes. So it's very specific. It's just rats. I mean, I am practically running with the mop. I don't look in the dark corners, I don't move anything I don't have to, I just do the bare minimum as fast as humanly possible to get out of there. I feel so scared the whole time, just this awful, tight knot in my stomach, jumping at every little shadow or creak the building makes. I'm just waiting for something to dart out at my boots. So, yeah, I don't even need to see an actual rat. Just knowing it's a place where they *could* be is enough to make me an absolute nervous wreck.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar fear response when thinking about other rodents, like mice, even if you said they don't bother you as much as rats?\n\nPatient: No, actually. Not at all. It’s funny you ask that, because people always assume, you know, if you hate one rodent, you must hate them all. But mice? Mice really don't do that to me. I mean, they’re a nuisance, obviously, especially in my line of work, but they don't give me that... that blinding panic. \n\nLike, just to give you an example, maybe two or three years ago, I was cleaning out this old supply closet on the third floor of the municipal building. It was packed with old files and junk. I moved this heavy box of paper towels, and a tiny little field mouse just scurried right over the toe of my work boot. And you know what I did? I just jumped back a little, maybe let out a little gasp, and went, 'Shoo! Get out of here!' And that was it. My heart didn't start pounding, I didn't get dizzy, I didn't drop my things and run for the door. I just thought, 'Ugh, gross, I need to leave a note for maintenance to put down a trap.' It was just an annoyance. \n\nBecause, I mean, mice are little, you know? They have those big round ears and they just want to hide from you. They're almost... well, I won't say cute, but they aren't monsters. But rats... rats are just entirely different. They're huge, they have those thick, scaly, disgusting tails, and they look at you like they aren't even scared of you. Like they'd just as soon bite you as run away. So no, thinking about mice doesn't bother me in the slightest. I can see a mouse on TV, or even think about one being in the wall, and I'm totally fine. \n\nEven squirrels! People say squirrels are just rats with fuzzy tails, right? But squirrels don't bother me one bit. I actually leave peanuts out for the squirrels on my back porch in the mornings. My husband thinks it's completely hilarious, given how I act about rats, but it's just not the same thing in my brain at all. It is exclusively, one hundred percent, just rats that make me feel like my world is ending.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So I called her and made up some excuse about a sudden migraine and went home. I felt terrible about it, and she was really disappointed. I think she knew I was lying, too. \n\nAnd then there's my husband. He tries to be patient, he really does, but I know it wears on him. I turn down any routes with laundry areas, too, especially in those older buildings where the machines are down in some damp cellar, or if the job means I have to take the heavy bags out to the alley dumpsters. I avoid all of it whenever I possibly can. I'll make up excuses, I'll trade shifts with the newer girls, whatever it takes. \n\nBut, you know, sometimes you just don't have a choice. Sometimes we're short-staffed, or there's a massive spill that has to be dealt with right then and there, or the boss is watching, and I'm forced to go clean one of those areas. And when that happens... it is just agonizing. I'm terrified before I even walk down the stairs. If I absolutely have to go in, I always, always keep the door propped wide open. I actually carry this heavy rubber wedge in my supply cart just for that, so I know I have a clear escape route and I won't get trapped down there. \n\nAnd then I just rush through the work. I mean, I am practically running with the mop. I don't look in the dark corners, I don't move anything I don't have to, I just do the bare minimum as fast as humanly possible to get out of there. I feel so scared the whole time, just this awful, tight knot in my stomach, jumping at every little shadow or creak the building makes. I'm just waiting for something to dart out at my boots. So, yeah, I don't even need to see an actual rat. Just knowing it's a place where they *could* be is enough to make me an absolute nervous wreck.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar fear response when thinking about other rodents, like mice, even if you said they don't bother you as much as rats?\n\nPatient: No, actually. Not at all. It’s funny you ask that, because people always assume, you know, if you hate one rodent, you must hate them all. But mice? Mice really don't do that to me. I mean, they’re a nuisance, obviously, especially in my line of work, but they don't give me that... that blinding panic. \n\nLike, just to give you an example, maybe two or three years ago, I was cleaning out this old supply closet on the third floor of the municipal building. It was packed with old files and junk. I moved this heavy box of paper towels, and a tiny little field mouse just scurried right over the toe of my work boot. And you know what I did? I just jumped back a little, maybe let out a little gasp, and went, 'Shoo! Get out of here!' And that was it. My heart didn't start pounding, I didn't get dizzy, I didn't drop my things and run for the door. I just thought, 'Ugh, gross, I need to leave a note for maintenance to put down a trap.' It was just an annoyance. \n\nBecause, I mean, mice are little, you know? They have those big round ears and they just want to hide from you. They're almost... well, I won't say cute, but they aren't monsters. But rats... rats are just entirely different. They're huge, they have those thick, scaly, disgusting tails, and they look at you like they aren't even scared of you. Like they'd just as soon bite you as run away. So no, thinking about mice doesn't bother me in the slightest. I can see a mouse on TV, or even think about one being in the wall, and I'm totally fine. \n\nEven squirrels! People say squirrels are just rats with fuzzy tails, right? But squirrels don't bother me one bit. I actually leave peanuts out for the squirrels on my back porch in the mornings. My husband thinks it's completely hilarious, given how I act about rats, but it's just not the same thing in my brain at all. It is exclusively, one hundred percent, just rats that make me feel like my world is ending.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your fear of rats ever caused you to avoid social gatherings or events because you thought rats might be present?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve had to refuse or delay cleaning assignments in certain buildings, and my supervisor has started questioning whether I can be counted on for regular routes. I’m worried this will cost me better shifts or a chance at a lead position.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, social gatherings... not really, no. I mean, I mostly just go over to my sister's house, or maybe my husband and I will go out to a nice, well-lit restaurant for dinner, so I don't really have to worry about them being there. I suppose if someone invited me to, I don't know, a picnic in a city park right next to the storm drains at dusk, then absolutely I would make up an excuse not to go. But my social life is mostly fine. 

But where it really, truly messes up my life, doctor—where it's actually ruining things for me—is my job. I know I mentioned earlier that I avoid certain routes, but it’s gotten so much worse than just trading a shift here or there. It’s gotten to the point where I’ve had to flat-out refuse or severely delay my cleaning assignments in certain buildings. Just last Tuesday, I was supposed to cover the downtown annex, which is this old brick place notorious for... well, you know. And I just couldn't do it. I sat in my car in the parking lot for forty-five minutes, just staring at the basement door, shaking, and finally, I had to call dispatch and tell them I couldn't go in. I made up some excuse about my stomach, but I think they know.

My supervisor, Gary—he’s usually a pretty understanding guy, we've worked together for over ten years—but he has really started questioning whether I can even be counted on for regular routes anymore. He actually pulled me aside into his office the other day with this very serious look on his face, and he asked me if I was, quote, "up to the demands" of the job. Which just broke my heart, because I work so hard! I take so much pride in my work, you know? I can get a linoleum floor to shine like glass. 

But because of this... this whole rat thing, I am terrified it’s going to cost me dearly. There's a lead position opening up next month for the day shift, which would mean better pay, better hours, and no more night shifts where every single shadow looks like a rat. I've been working toward that promotion for three years. But I'm so worried I'm going to lose my chance at it now. Or even just lose my good shifts entirely, because why would Gary promote someone who refuses to go into half the buildings on the roster? It's just... it's taking over my livelihood, it really is, and I don't know how to fix it before I ruin my career.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi doctor. Thank you for fitting me in. I, um... I'm here because things have just gotten completely out of hand at work. I'm a building cleaner, I've been doing janitorial work for a long time now, mostly in these older commercial buildings downtown. And... well, it's the rats. Or, really, even just the *thought* of seeing a rat. \n\nIt has gotten so incredibly bad lately that I literally can't do parts of my job anymore. I mean, I'll be pushing my cart down to the basement level or heading toward the main trash room at the end of my shift, and I just... I can't go in. I stand there at the door and I just can't make myself do it. And the frustrating part—the really maddening part—is that I know better! I tell myself over and over, I know it’s probably just an old building, you know? It's just a trash room. It's not actually dangerous to go in there and empty the bins. \n\nBut I just picture one scurrying out from under a dumpster, or I think I hear a scratch, and my whole body just refuses to move. I've had to start asking the new guy on my shift to cover the lower levels for me, making up excuses, and I can't keep doing that without getting fired. I've been at this job for years and I can't afford to lose it, but I just don't know how to stop being so terrified over something that probably isn't even there most of the time...\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or places outside of work where you avoid going because you might see a rat?\n\nPatient: Oh, everywhere. It’s definitely not just at my job anymore, it’s really bleeding into my everyday life and dictating where I can and can't go. I mean, I will completely turn down any walking routes or going into places if they involve basements, or those creepy little storage rooms, or even communal laundry areas. Even alley dumpsters! Like, if I'm walking home from the grocery store or running errands, I will literally cross the street and walk three or four blocks out of my way just to avoid walking past an alley with a dumpster, because my mind just immediately goes to what might be hiding in there. \n\nI try to avoid them whenever I possibly can, but you know, sometimes you just can't. And if I can't avoid it—like if I’m absolutely forced to clean one of those areas, whether it's the laundry room at my own apartment building or one of the storage rooms on my route at work that I couldn't get the new guy to cover for me—I have to leave the door wide open. I'll find a brick or a heavy trash can, whatever I can find, really, just to wedge that door open so I know I have a clear escape route. And then I just rush through it. I'm practically running, just rushing through the sweeping or the mopping as fast as I possibly can, not even doing a very thorough job if I'm being honest, and I just feel completely scared the whole entire time. I'm just on edge, staring at the corners, waiting for something to dart out at my feet. It’s just so exhausting, doctor, having to constantly map out my entire day just to avoid a laundry room or a dumpster, and then being absolutely terrified when I'm forced to deal with one.\n\nDoctor: Is it just rats that you're afraid of, or are there other animals or things that trigger a similar fear response in you?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's really just the rats. I mean, I'm not a fan of spiders or anything, but who is, right? If I see a spider, I just grab a paper towel and squish it, or I'll ask someone else to get it if it's a big one. It's nothing like this. And mice... well, mice are gross, sure, but they don't give me that same absolute panic. I can handle a mouse trap if I absolutely have to, though I prefer not to. \n\nBut rats... it's just something about them. The way they look, the way they move, those long tails... just thinking about it makes my skin crawl. I don't know what it is, honestly. I've never been bitten by one or anything like that. I didn't have some traumatic childhood experience with a pet rat or anything. It's just this deep, overpowering terror that I can't shake. \n\nLike, the other day, I saw a squirrel run across the sidewalk, and for a split second, my brain registered \"rat\" and my heart just stopped. I literally froze in my tracks until I realized what it was. And pigeons, you know, people call them rats with wings, but they don't bother me at all. I can sit on a park bench and eat my lunch with pigeons walking right up to my shoes. So it's very specific. It's just rats. Nothing else makes me feel like my chest is caving in and I need to run for my life. Just them.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I guess not directly? Like, nobody has stopped talking to me because I'm afraid of rats, if that's what you mean. My family knows about it, obviously. My sister, she thinks I'm just being dramatic, which is so frustrating because she doesn't understand that it's not a choice. I'm not just acting out for attention, you know? And my friends... well, my friends are mostly pretty understanding, I suppose. \n\nBut it does cause problems, just because of how much I have to avoid things. Like, last month, my friend Sarah invited me over for a barbecue at her new place. It was a really nice house, but to get to the backyard, you had to walk down this side path that was right next to a row of big, metal dumpsters for the apartment building next door. And I just... I couldn't do it. I stood at the front of her house, looking down that path, and I knew, I just *knew* there were rats in those dumpsters. I could practically hear them. So I called her and made up some excuse about a sudden migraine and went home. I felt terrible about it, and she was really disappointed. I think she knew I was lying, too. \n\nAnd then there's my husband. He tries to be patient, he really does, but I know it wears on him. Like, if we're out walking in the evening, I'll suddenly grab his arm and drag him across the street because I see a shadow near a storm drain. Or I'll refuse to go down to our own basement to get the holiday decorations, so he has to do it all by himself. He never complains, but I can see the look on his face sometimes, just this... exhaustion. Like he's tired of having to accommodate this ridiculous fear of mine. So, I don't know if it's ruining my relationships or anything, but it definitely puts a strain on things. It makes me feel like a burden, honestly, having to constantly explain why I can't go certain places or do certain things. It's just embarrassing, having to admit that a grown woman is terrified of a rodent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, like a panic attack, when you thought about or encountered rats?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yes. It's not a slow build-up, you know? It's not like I start getting a little nervous and then it gets worse. It is instant. It's like flipping a switch. \n\nJust last week, I was cleaning the first-floor hallway of this one building on my route—an older building, lots of exposed pipes and weird little nooks. I was just mopping, minding my own business, thinking about what I was going to make for dinner, when I heard this... scratching sound. Just a tiny, little scratch-scratch-scratch coming from behind the baseboard near the radiator pipe. And I didn't even see anything! I just heard the sound, and my brain instantly screamed *rat*. \n\nMy whole body just reacted right away. It's like I couldn't even control it. My heart started pounding so hard and so fast I thought it was going to actually crack my ribs. I could hear my pulse roaring in my ears. And my hands... my hands started shaking so violently I actually dropped the mop handle. It just clattered to the floor. I couldn't breathe right, my chest got all tight, and I just had this overwhelming, blinding urge that I had to get out of there. Like, right that second. Before I could even think straight or rationalize that it might just be the pipes settling or a mouse or something, I was already backing away toward the heavy glass front doors. \n\nIt happens if I see something move out of the corner of my eye near a pipe, or if I hear a rustle in a trash bag I'm about to tie up. It's this sudden, massive wave of pure panic that just crashes over me in seconds. I get dizzy, I sweat, and I just feel this absolute, terrifying certainty that if I don't run away immediately, something horrible is going to happen to me. It takes me sometimes twenty or thirty minutes just sitting in my car with the doors locked and the AC blasting to finally get my breathing back to normal and stop shaking after something like that happens. It's just awful, doctor. It really is.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the actual presence of rats or also by the possibility that rats might be in a particular location?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely the possibility. Honestly, just the thought that they *might* be there is enough to completely dictate how I do my job. You know, being a building cleaner, you get assigned all sorts of different zones or routes, and I’ve had to get really, really strict about what I’ll accept. If my supervisor tries to hand me a schedule that includes basements, or those creepy, cluttered storage rooms where people just dump old mattresses and cardboard boxes... I just turn them down. I have to. I turn down any routes with laundry areas, too, especially in those older buildings where the machines are down in some damp cellar, or if the job means I have to take the heavy bags out to the alley dumpsters. I avoid all of it whenever I possibly can. I'll make up excuses, I'll trade shifts with the newer girls, whatever it takes. \n\nBut, you know, sometimes you just don't have a choice. Sometimes we're short-staffed, or there's a massive spill that has to be dealt with right then and there, or the boss is watching, and I'm forced to go clean one of those areas. And when that happens... it is just agonizing. I'm terrified before I even walk down the stairs. If I absolutely have to go in, I always, always keep the door propped wide open. I actually carry this heavy rubber wedge in my supply cart just for that, so I know I have a clear escape route and I won't get trapped down there. \n\nAnd then I just rush through the work. So I called her and made up some excuse about a sudden migraine and went home. I felt terrible about it, and she was really disappointed. I think she knew I was lying, too. \n\nAnd then there's my husband. He tries to be patient, he really does, but I know it wears on him. Like, if we're out walking in the evening, I'll suddenly grab his arm and drag him across the street because I see a shadow near a storm drain. Or I'll refuse to go down to our own basement to get the holiday decorations, so he has to do it all by himself. He never complains, but I can see the look on his face sometimes, just this... exhaustion. Like he's tired of having to accommodate this ridiculous fear of mine. So, I don't know if it's ruining my relationships or anything, but it definitely puts a strain on things. It makes me feel like a burden, honestly, having to constantly explain why I can't go certain places or do certain things. It's just embarrassing, having to admit that a grown woman is terrified of a rodent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense feeling of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, like a panic attack, when you thought about or encountered rats?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yes. It's not a slow build-up, you know? It's not like I start getting a little nervous and then it gets worse. It is instant. It's like flipping a switch. \n\nJust last week, I was cleaning the first-floor hallway of this one building on my route—an older building, lots of exposed pipes and weird little nooks. I was just mopping, minding my own business, thinking about what I was going to make for dinner, when I heard this... scratching sound. Just a tiny, little scratch-scratch-scratch coming from behind the baseboard near the radiator pipe. And I didn't even see anything! I just heard the sound, and my brain instantly screamed *rat*. \n\nMy whole body just reacted right away. It's like I couldn't even control it. My heart started pounding so hard and so fast I thought it was going to actually crack my ribs. I could hear my pulse roaring in my ears. And my hands... my hands started shaking so violently I actually dropped the mop handle. It just clattered to the floor. I couldn't breathe right, my chest got all tight, and I just had this overwhelming, blinding urge that I had to get out of there. Like, right that second. Before I could even think straight or rationalize that it might just be the pipes settling or a mouse or something, I was already backing away toward the heavy glass front doors. \n\nIt happens if I see something move out of the corner of my eye near a pipe, or if I hear a rustle in a trash bag I'm about to tie up. It's this sudden, massive wave of pure panic that just crashes over me in seconds. I get dizzy, I sweat, and I just feel this absolute, terrifying certainty that if I don't run away immediately, something horrible is going to happen to me. It takes me sometimes twenty or thirty minutes just sitting in my car with the doors locked and the AC blasting to finally get my breathing back to normal and stop shaking after something like that happens. It's just awful, doctor. It really is.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the actual presence of rats or also by the possibility that rats might be in a particular location?\n\nPatient: Oh, it’s definitely the possibility. Honestly, just the thought that they *might* be there is enough to completely dictate how I do my job. You know, being a building cleaner, you get assigned all sorts of different zones or routes, and I’ve had to get really, really strict about what I’ll accept. If my supervisor tries to hand me a schedule that includes basements, or those creepy, cluttered storage rooms where people just dump old mattresses and cardboard boxes... I just turn them down. I have to. I turn down any routes with laundry areas, too, especially in those older buildings where the machines are down in some damp cellar, or if the job means I have to take the heavy bags out to the alley dumpsters. I avoid all of it whenever I possibly can. I'll make up excuses, I'll trade shifts with the newer girls, whatever it takes. \n\nBut, you know, sometimes you just don't have a choice. Sometimes we're short-staffed, or there's a massive spill that has to be dealt with right then and there, or the boss is watching, and I'm forced to go clean one of those areas. And when that happens... it is just agonizing. I'm terrified before I even walk down the stairs. If I absolutely have to go in, I always, always keep the door propped wide open. I actually carry this heavy rubber wedge in my supply cart just for that, so I know I have a clear escape route and I won't get trapped down there. \n\nAnd then I just rush through the work. I mean, I am practically running with the mop. I don't look in the dark corners, I don't move anything I don't have to, I just do the bare minimum as fast as humanly possible to get out of there. I feel so scared the whole time, just this awful, tight knot in my stomach, jumping at every little shadow or creak the building makes. I'm just waiting for something to dart out at my boots. So, yeah, I don't even need to see an actual rat. Just knowing it's a place where they *could* be is enough to make me an absolute nervous wreck.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar fear response when thinking about other rodents, like mice, even if you said they don't bother you as much as rats?\n\nPatient: No, actually. Not at all. It’s funny you ask that, because people always assume, you know, if you hate one rodent, you must hate them all. But mice? Mice really don't do that to me. I mean, they’re a nuisance, obviously, especially in my line of work, but they don't give me that... that blinding panic. \n\nLike, just to give you an example, maybe two or three years ago, I was cleaning out this old supply closet on the third floor of the municipal building. It was packed with old files and junk. I moved this heavy box of paper towels, and a tiny little field mouse just scurried right over the toe of my work boot. And you know what I did? I just jumped back a little, maybe let out a little gasp, and went, 'Shoo! Get out of here!' And that was it. My heart didn't start pounding, I didn't get dizzy, I didn't drop my things and run for the door. I just thought, 'Ugh, gross, I need to leave a note for maintenance to put down a trap.' It was just an annoyance. \n\nBecause, I mean, mice are little, you know? They have those big round ears and they just want to hide from you. They're almost... well, I won't say cute, but they aren't monsters. But rats... rats are just entirely different. They're huge, they have those thick, scaly, disgusting tails, and they look at you like they aren't even scared of you. Like they'd just as soon bite you as run away. So no, thinking about mice doesn't bother me in the slightest. I can see a mouse on TV, or even think about one being in the wall, and I'm totally fine. \n\nEven squirrels! People say squirrels are just rats with fuzzy tails, right? But squirrels don't bother me one bit. I actually leave peanuts out for the squirrels on my back porch in the mornings. My husband thinks it's completely hilarious, given how I act about rats, but it's just not the same thing in my brain at all. It is exclusively, one hundred percent, just rats that make me feel like my world is ending.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats ever caused you to avoid social gatherings or events because you thought rats might be present?\n\nPatient: Well, social gatherings... not really, no. I mean, I mostly just go over to my sister's house, or maybe my husband and I will go out to a nice, well-lit restaurant for dinner, so I don't really have to worry about them being there. I suppose if someone invited me to, I don't know, a picnic in a city park right next to the storm drains at dusk, then absolutely I would make up an excuse not to go. But my social life is mostly fine. \n\nBut where it really, truly messes up my life, doctor—where it's actually ruining things for me—is my job. I know I mentioned earlier that I avoid certain routes, but it’s gotten so much worse than just trading a shift here or there. It’s gotten to the point where I’ve had to flat-out refuse or severely delay my cleaning assignments in certain buildings. Just last Tuesday, I was supposed to cover the downtown annex, which is this old brick place notorious for... well, you know. And I just couldn't do it. I sat in my car in the parking lot for forty-five minutes, just staring at the basement door, shaking, and finally, I had to call dispatch and tell them I couldn't go in. I made up some excuse about my stomach, but I think they know.\n\nMy supervisor, Gary—he’s usually a pretty understanding guy, we've worked together for over ten years—but he has really started questioning whether I can even be counted on for regular routes anymore. He actually pulled me aside into his office the other day with this very serious look on his face, and he asked me if I was, quote, \"up to the demands\" of the job. Which just broke my heart, because I work so hard! I take so much pride in my work, you know? I can get a linoleum floor to shine like glass. \n\nBut because of this... this whole rat thing, I am terrified it’s going to cost me dearly. There's a lead position opening up next month for the day shift, which would mean better pay, better hours, and no more night shifts where every single shadow looks like a rat. I've been working toward that promotion for three years. But I'm so worried I'm going to lose my chance at it now. Or even just lose my good shifts entirely, because why would Gary promote someone who refuses to go into half the buildings on the roster? It's just... it's taking over my livelihood, it really is, and I don't know how to fix it before I ruin my career.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

At home? No, no, thank goodness. My home is... well, it’s my sanctuary, really. You see, since I clean for a living, you can probably imagine exactly how spotless I keep my own house. We live in this little single-story ranch we bought about fifteen years ago, and I am telling you, there is absolutely no way a rat is getting in there. I just wouldn't allow it. My husband, he knows exactly how terrified I am, bless his heart, so a few years back he actually went around the entire outside of the house, the whole foundation, with that expanding foam sealant stuff. He filled in every single tiny crack or gap he could find, even around the pipes where the garden hose hooks up, just to give me peace of mind. 

So, because of all that, I don't really have to avoid doing anything around the house. I do the laundry just fine, but then again, our washer and dryer are right in the mudroom off the kitchen. It’s all bright and on the ground floor, not down in some dark, damp cellar like the buildings I have to clean. And taking out the trash—well, my husband usually rolls the big bins down to the curb on Sunday nights, but if I have to take a kitchen bag out during the week, it doesn't bother me. We had these heavy-duty motion-sensor LED floodlights installed right over the side door last spring. The second you even touch the doorknob, the whole driveway lights up like it's high noon, so there are absolutely no shadows for anything to hide in. 

I guess what I mean is, home is the one place I can actually just breathe. I don't have to constantly look over my shoulder or worry about what's behind the water heater. I don't avoid the garage, I don't avoid the yard. I can just be normal. It’s really just when I step foot on the clock at work and have to go into those old, neglected buildings that everything completely falls apart. But at home? No, I do all my everyday chores without any problems at all.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi doctor. Thank you for fitting me in. I, um... I'm here because things have just gotten completely out of hand at work. I'm a building cleaner, I've been doing janitorial work for a long time now, mostly in these older commercial buildings downtown. And... well, it's the rats. Or, really, even just the *thought* of seeing a rat. \n\nIt has gotten so incredibly bad lately that I literally can't do parts of my job anymore. I mean, I'll be pushing my cart down to the basement level or heading toward the main trash room at the end of my shift, and I just... I can't go in. I stand there at the door and I just can't make myself do it. And the frustrating part—the really maddening part—is that I know better! I tell myself over and over, I know it’s probably just an old building, you know? It's just a trash room.
