I avoid all of it whenever I possibly can. I'll make up excuses, I'll trade shifts with the newer girls, whatever it takes. \n\nBut, you know, sometimes you just don't have a choice. Sometimes we're short-staffed, or there's a massive spill that has to be dealt with right then and there, or the boss is watching, and I'm forced to go clean one of those areas. And when that happens... it is just agonizing. I'm terrified before I even walk down the stairs. If I absolutely have to go in, I always, always keep the door propped wide open. I actually carry this heavy rubber wedge in my supply cart just for that, so I know I have a clear escape route and I won't get trapped down there. \n\nAnd then I just rush through the work. I mean, I am practically running with the mop. I don't look in the dark corners, I don't move anything I don't have to, I just do the bare minimum as fast as humanly possible to get out of there. I feel so scared the whole time, just this awful, tight knot in my stomach, jumping at every little shadow or creak the building makes. I'm just waiting for something to dart out at my boots. So, yeah, I don't even need to see an actual rat. Just knowing it's a place where they *could* be is enough to make me an absolute nervous wreck.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a similar fear response when thinking about other rodents, like mice, even if you said they don't bother you as much as rats?\n\nPatient: No, actually. Not at all. It’s funny you ask that, because people always assume, you know, if you hate one rodent, you must hate them all. But mice? Mice really don't do that to me. I mean, they’re a nuisance, obviously, especially in my line of work, but they don't give me that... that blinding panic. \n\nLike, just to give you an example, maybe two or three years ago, I was cleaning out this old supply closet on the third floor of the municipal building. It was packed with old files and junk. I moved this heavy box of paper towels, and a tiny little field mouse just scurried right over the toe of my work boot. And you know what I did? I just jumped back a little, maybe let out a little gasp, and went, 'Shoo! Get out of here!' And that was it. My heart didn't start pounding, I didn't get dizzy, I didn't drop my things and run for the door. I just thought, 'Ugh, gross, I need to leave a note for maintenance to put down a trap.' It was just an annoyance. \n\nBecause, I mean, mice are little, you know? They have those big round ears and they just want to hide from you. They're almost... well, I won't say cute, but they aren't monsters. But rats... rats are just entirely different. They're huge, they have those thick, scaly, disgusting tails, and they look at you like they aren't even scared of you. Like they'd just as soon bite you as run away. So no, thinking about mice doesn't bother me in the slightest. I can see a mouse on TV, or even think about one being in the wall, and I'm totally fine. \n\nEven squirrels! People say squirrels are just rats with fuzzy tails, right? But squirrels don't bother me one bit. I actually leave peanuts out for the squirrels on my back porch in the mornings. My husband thinks it's completely hilarious, given how I act about rats, but it's just not the same thing in my brain at all. It is exclusively, one hundred percent, just rats that make me feel like my world is ending.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats ever caused you to avoid social gatherings or events because you thought rats might be present?\n\nPatient: Well, social gatherings... not really, no. I mean, I mostly just go over to my sister's house, or maybe my husband and I will go out to a nice, well-lit restaurant for dinner, so I don't really have to worry about them being there. I suppose if someone invited me to, I don't know, a picnic in a city park right next to the storm drains at dusk, then absolutely I would make up an excuse not to go. But my social life is mostly fine. \n\nBut where it really, truly messes up my life, doctor—where it's actually ruining things for me—is my job. I know I mentioned earlier that I avoid certain routes, but it’s gotten so much worse than just trading a shift here or there. It’s gotten to the point where I’ve had to flat-out refuse or severely delay my cleaning assignments in certain buildings. Just last Tuesday, I was supposed to cover the downtown annex, which is this old brick place notorious for... well, you know. And I just couldn't do it. I sat in my car in the parking lot for forty-five minutes, just staring at the basement door, shaking, and finally, I had to call dispatch and tell them I couldn't go in. I made up some excuse about my stomach, but I think they know.\n\nMy supervisor, Gary—he’s usually a pretty understanding guy, we've worked together for over ten years—but he has really started questioning whether I can even be counted on for regular routes anymore. He actually pulled me aside into his office the other day with this very serious look on his face, and he asked me if I was, quote, \"up to the demands\" of the job. Which just broke my heart, because I work so hard! I take so much pride in my work, you know? I can get a linoleum floor to shine like glass. \n\nBut because of this... this whole rat thing, I am terrified it’s going to cost me dearly. There's a lead position opening up next month for the day shift, which would mean better pay, better hours, and no more night shifts where every single shadow looks like a rat. I've been working toward that promotion for three years. But I'm so worried I'm going to lose my chance at it now. Or even just lose my good shifts entirely, because why would Gary promote someone who refuses to go into half the buildings on the roster? It's just... it's taking over my livelihood, it really is, and I don't know how to fix it before I ruin my career.\n\nDoctor: Are there any everyday activities at home that you avoid because of your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: At home? No, no, thank goodness. My home is... well, it’s my sanctuary, really. You see, since I clean for a living, you can probably imagine exactly how spotless I keep my own house. We live in this little single-story ranch we bought about fifteen years ago, and I am telling you, there is absolutely no way a rat is getting in there. I just wouldn't allow it. My husband, he knows exactly how terrified I am, bless his heart, so a few years back he actually went around the entire outside of the house, the whole foundation, with that expanding foam sealant stuff. He filled in every single tiny crack or gap he could find, even around the pipes where the garden hose hooks up, just to give me peace of mind. \n\nSo, because of all that, I don't really have to avoid doing anything around the house. I do the laundry just fine, but then again, our washer and dryer are right in the mudroom off the kitchen. It’s all bright and on the ground floor, not down in some dark, damp cellar like the buildings I have to clean. And taking out the trash—well, my husband usually rolls the big bins down to the curb on Sunday nights, but if I have to take a kitchen bag out during the week, it doesn't bother me. We had these heavy-duty motion-sensor LED floodlights installed right over the side door last spring. The second you even touch the doorknob, the whole driveway lights up like it's high noon, so there are absolutely no shadows for anything to hide in. \n\nI guess what I mean is, home is the one place I can actually just breathe. I don't have to constantly look over my shoulder or worry about what's behind the water heater. I don't avoid the garage, I don't avoid the yard. I can just be normal. It’s really just when I step foot on the clock at work and have to go into those old, neglected buildings that everything completely falls apart. But at home? No, I do all my everyday chores without any problems at all.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this intense fear of rats?\n\nPatient: Well, it hasn't been my whole life, if that's what you're wondering. It’s been... gosh, right about two years now. Two years of this absolute nightmare. And I know exactly when it started, too, because it was right around the time my husband and I had to temporarily move out of our house. We had this awful water damage situation in the guest bathroom of our ranch—just a complete disaster, pipes burst, the insurance adjusters took absolutely forever—so we had to pack up a bunch of our things and move into this older apartment building on the east side of town for about four months while the contractors gutted our place. \n\nAnd that apartment building... oh, it was just awful. I mean, the rent was covered by insurance, which was fine, but the tenants there, the neighbors you'd run into in the halls or down in the communal laundry room, they just kept talking about rats. Constantly. You'd go down to check the mail, and some lady from the second floor would be complaining to the super about hearing scratching in the walls, or someone else would be talking about seeing a huge one by the dumpsters out back. \n\nI never actually saw one in our unit, thank God, because I scrubbed that temporary place top to bottom with bleach the second we got the keys, but just the talk... the constant, endless talking about them being in the walls, in the basement, in the drop ceilings. It just got into my head. I’d be lying awake at two in the morning in this strange bed, just listening to the old radiator pipes clanking, and every single little noise made my chest tighten up. I'd just lay there thinking, 'Is that them? Are they chewing through the drywall?' \n\nAnd it just spiraled from there. Even after we finally moved back into our own house—which, like I said, my husband sealed up tight as a drum—the fear just followed me. It followed me right to my job, and it just kept getting bigger and bigger in my mind. So yeah, it's been about two years of just... looking over my shoulder, all because we had to stay in that awful apartment building with those stories.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your fear of rats is controlling your life and limiting your ability to participate in activities or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as my relationships go, like with my husband or my sister, no, it really hasn't limited those at all. They’ve been just wonderful and so patient with me, especially my husband with all the sealing up of the house he did. But when you ask if it's controlling my life? Oh, doctor, absolutely. One hundred percent. It is completely running the show, and like I was pouring my heart out to you about a few minutes ago, it all comes down to my job. I know I already brought it up, but it’s just weighing on me so heavily because my work is such a huge part of who I am, and I just can't stop obsessing over how badly this is messing everything up.\n\nWhen I think about how it's controlling things, I just keep going back to how I’ve had to refuse or delay my cleaning assignments in certain buildings. It’s not just a one-time thing anymore. Sometimes I don't even flat-out refuse right away, I'll just sit there in my car with the engine running, gripping the steering wheel, looking at the clock and telling myself, 'Okay, just five more minutes, then I'll go inside.' And then twenty minutes go by, and then forty-five, and my whole nightly schedule is completely thrown off because I just can't bring myself to open the door to those older properties. \n\nAnd that conversation with my supervisor, Gary... I just can't get it out of my head. The fact that he has actually started questioning whether I can be counted on for my regular routes anymore... it just stings. It really, truly does. Because I am a reliable person! I've always been the one they call when there's a tough spill or a floor that needs extra buffing. But when my brain takes over and I'm paralyzed in that parking lot, reliability goes right out the window. \n\nI am just so incredibly worried that this is going to cost me my chance at that lead position. Or that it'll cost me the better shifts I've worked so hard to earn over the last ten years. That lead role would change everything for me—daytime hours, a little bump in pay, no more creeping around dark annexes by myself—and the thought that this... this absolute terror is going to sabotage my career? It’s just devastating. So yes, to answer your question, it is absolutely controlling my life, because it's holding my entire livelihood hostage, and I just feel so powerless to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities or routines outside of work that you've had to change or avoid because of your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? Well, honestly, like I was saying about the house, my regular day-to-day life outside of my shifts is mostly fine. I go to the grocery store in broad daylight, I run my errands, I visit my sister... it's all okay. But... well, I know you're asking about outside of work, but my daily routine really *is* my work, you know? That's where I spend most of my waking hours, and that's where my routine has just been completely turned upside down. I just can't stop thinking about it, doctor, because it dictates every single thing I do on my shift now. \n\nI mean, I will literally turn down any cleaning routes if I know they involve basements, or those big, cluttered storage rooms, or communal laundry areas. And alley dumpsters! Oh, don't even get me started on alley dumpsters. If a building requires me to take the trash out to a dark alley, I will do everything in my power to avoid it. I'll ask Gary to swap my route, I'll beg the other girls to trade buildings with me, whatever it takes. \n\nBut the worst part is when I can't get out of it. Sometimes Gary says, 'No, we're short-staffed, you have to do the east building tonight,' and I'm just forced to clean one of those areas. When that happens, I am just an absolute nervous wreck. If I have to go into a basement or a storage room, I absolutely have to keep the door propped wide open—I'll wedge my heavy yellow mop bucket against it, or a chair, whatever I can find, just to make sure I have an escape route and I'm not trapped in there. \n\nAnd then I just rush through the whole area. I am practically running with the broom, just sweeping and wiping as fast as humanly possible, which isn't even how I like to clean! I like to be thorough, I take pride in my work! But I just can't do it down there. I feel so incredibly scared the entire time I'm in there, just waiting for something to dart out at me from behind a box or a washing machine. My heart is just pounding in my throat from the second I walk in until the second I leave, and I don't feel safe until I'm back out in the main hallways. It's just exhausting having to work like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a fear response similar to the one you have towards rats when encountering other animals or objects that are not rats, but might resemble them or be found in similar environments?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s funny you ask that, because... yes. Oh, absolutely yes. It doesn't even have to actually *be* one of them for me to completely lose my mind. It could be literally anything that just... tricks my brain for a split second. Because I'm already on such high alert, you know? Like I was saying about being in those basements and constantly looking over my shoulder, my nerves are just completely shot by the time I clock in. \n\nSo, if I'm sweeping up and I just hear a little scratching noise near a wall—which, in these old buildings I clean, could just be the settling of the foundation or, I don't know, a loose piece of siding blowing in the wind outside—or if I just see something move out of the corner of my eye by a pipe, my whole body reacts right away. It’s instantaneous. I don't even have time to register what it actually is, my body just takes over. My heart starts pounding so hard I can literally feel it thumping in my throat, my hands start to shake so badly I can barely hold onto my broom handle, and I just get this overwhelming, intense feeling that I have to get out. I have to get out of that room before I can even think straight. \n\nJust last Tuesday, I was in the utility closet on the third floor of the medical annex, and there was this crumpled-up brown paper towel that someone had missed the trash can with, and it was just sitting there in the shadows near the baseboard. And I swear to you, doctor, the air conditioning kicked on and made it flutter just a tiny bit, and I practically jumped out of my own skin. I was halfway down the hall, shaking like a leaf, gasping for air, before my brain even processed that it was just a paper towel. \n\nOr, you know, a stray dry leaf blowing across the pavement when I'm trying to take the recycling out to the curb. Or a piece of dark lint by a radiator. It doesn't matter that it's not actually a rat. The fear is exactly the same. It's like my body doesn't wait for my eyes to double-check, it just hits the panic button immediately. It is just so exhausting living on this kind of hair-trigger all the time, constantly being terrified of shadows and sounds, and having to calm myself down from a full-blown panic over a piece of trash. It just wears you down to the bone, it really does.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or tasks that you used to enjoy or found important, but now avoid because of your fear of rats?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I was kind of touching on this a minute ago when you asked about my routine outside of work, but honestly, I don’t really have a lot of big hobbies or activities that I do anyway. I’m a pretty simple person, you know? I work my night shifts, I come home, I keep my own house spotless—which, thank goodness, my husband sealed up so tight I don't have to worry there—and I visit my sister. We still do our Sunday afternoon coffee on her back patio, rain or shine, and I still sit and do my crossword puzzles at the kitchen table in the mornings while I'm trying to wind down for sleep. None of that has been affected at all. I haven't stopped doing any of those things, so I can't say I'm avoiding anything like that. \n\nBut... well, I guess if you really think about it, the task I used to enjoy the most, the thing I found most important... was my job. I know, I know, people hear 'building cleaner' and they think it's just drudgery, but I loved it. I really did. There is such a peace in taking a messy, scuffed-up hallway and making it look brand new by the time the sun comes up. I used to put my headphones in, listen to my oldies station, and just get into this wonderful rhythm. It was satisfying! It gave me a real sense of purpose. \n\nAnd now? Now that's the thing I'm avoiding. I mean, I don't avoid the whole job, obviously, because I need the paycheck, but I avoid the parts of it that used to just be normal, everyday tasks. I don't put my headphones in anymore because I have to be listening for every little scratch or squeak. I don't get into a rhythm because I'm too busy propping doors open and looking over my shoulder. So, outside of work, no, I haven't given up any fun activities. But the one thing I really took pride in? The thing that actually made me feel good about my day? That’s what’s been ruined. And it just breaks my heart, doctor, it really does, because I don't have a lot of other things, and this fear is just bleeding all the joy right out of the one thing I did have.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of rats been consistently affecting your daily life and work over the past two years, or have there been periods where it was less bothersome?\n\nPatient: Oh, I really, really wish I could tell you that it comes and goes, or that I get some sort of break from it, but... no. No, it has been completely consistent. Every single day, every single shift. \n\nThis whole nightmare has been going on for about two years now, and it hasn't let up for a second. It all started right around the time we moved. We had just moved into this older apartment building—it’s one of those big, heavy brick buildings, you know, it has good bones but it’s definitely showing its age. We needed something a little more affordable, and it seemed fine at first. But almost the second we got our boxes unpacked, I started hearing the other tenants talking. \n\nYou'd just be down by the mailboxes or passing someone in the stairwell, and people just kept talking about rats. It was constant! Someone would say, \"Oh, make sure you don't leave your trash bags on the back porch, the rats are terrible this season,\" or they'd complain to the landlord about hearing scratching in the walls at night. I never even saw one there myself, thank god, but just hearing everyone talk about them... it did something to me. It just flipped this switch in my brain. \n\nAnd from that point on, about two years ago, the fear just completely took over. Like I told you earlier, my husband went absolutely crazy sealing up our apartment so I could feel safe at home, but the fear didn't stay at home. It followed me straight to work, and it has been sitting heavy on my chest ever since. There hasn't been a good month, or a good week, or even a single night where I felt like it was less bothersome. It’s just been two solid years of being terrified every time I pick up my mop."
